Helicopter Parenting Can Help Children Recover From Concussions
2026-09-20

Helicopter Parenting Can Help Children Recover From Concussions

TIME | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Concussions in children are alarmingly frequent, affecting over a million children in the U.S. each year. An estimated 50% of all concussions are undetected, unreported, and untreated. As the founder and director of a concussion clinic in New York, I see my fair share of concerned parents who want to do everything they can to help their kids heal from concussion. It’s a good instinct: Early and active intervention with targeted therapies is critical. From years of caring for children and adolescents with concussion, my team and I wondered if the parents most involved in their children’s recovery—some might call them “helicopter parents”—could be a key part of navigating a serious medical problem which, left untreated, may have devastating consequences. Our society has turned against helicopter parents, viewing them as unnecessarily controlling. But they are uniquely valuable as a temporary framework for healing. The treatment regimen for concussion involves drastic lifestyle changes surrounding their daily screen-time exposure, shifting circadian rhythms, intellectual stimulation, and the cognitive demands of school or work. Everything has to be turned down or dialed back, sometimes for weeks at a time. The brain heals gradually, and each lifestyle change removes a source of strain it would otherwise have to contend with while heeding a vigilant dietary and sleep schedule. I see often in my clinic that the children with very involved parents are the ones most likely to recover swiftly and without complications. After their child gets a concussion, these are the parents who diligently and methodically plan waking hours, sleeping hours, meal times, recreation, and exercise, and gradually adjust screen time. These strict parental accommodations are fundamental to recovery. Although the degree of supervision may be controversial to developmentally favored ideas of nurturing independence in adolescence, a brief “helicopter” approach in the context of youth brain injury can be remarkably effective. Parents of young children or young adults who are recovering from concussion must be involved from the start and commit to executing all aspects of the recovery plan while modeling positive coping styles. These interventions can also be effective for young adults in their 20s who had already adjusted to and embraced an independent life. One typical reaction of a parent to their twenty-something offspring could reasonably be that their child has to navigate their treatment and recovery on their own. Yet irrespective of their age or how long they had or had not lived at home, my team and I found, time and again, that it’s extremely beneficial to have a parent swoop in, micromanage a patient’s schedule, and deliberately revert to the past concern and meticulous care that they took on when their child was very young. The person they are caring for who has to adhere to the concussion regimen cannot easily follow the regimen without them. A young woman was hit in the back of her head in a boating accident, and suffered an “out of body” experience. Her father reluctantly traveled from far to come to her side, believing she was a young adult who could surely manage this invisible injury by herself. He found her to be different: she had confusion when calculating tips, struggled with maps, and complained of headaches in her entire head accompanied by eye pain. After a few days together, she reluctantly pulled out of the marathon she had signed up for and allowed her father to regulate her protein intake and remind her when it was her bedtime. Another young man was skiing and fell forward down a hill, landing 30 feet downhill.  Every time he looked at a screen, “it was the shape of a lightbulb, surrounded by tiny little pinwheels” and said “my mental compass was off and I could hear my internal monologue.” Here began the necessary dialogue with his family and his mother was soon at his side, ensuring that he took the correct vitamins at the correct time, cutting tablets into half when doses needed to be titrated, and counting his eye and neck exercises. No matter how motivated a concussed individual is to achieve recovery, they rarely have the capacity to follow the treatment regimen, which involves drastic lifestyle changes affecting their daily screen exposure, shifting circadian rhythms, intellectual stimulation, and the cognitive demands of school or work. The intriguing and undeniable result was a patient who recovered more swiftly and with fewer complications or setbacks when compared with their independent counterparts. The helicopter parent  actively reinforces the details, effectively guiding an incremental and purposeful exposure to stimuli. The parent also validates the recovery process as an active coach, facilitating and motivating the path ahead. Concussion recovery is best accomplished using a systematic and methodical approach. Each advancement toward complete recovery is closely calibrated whether it be bedtime, the number of minutes of breaks between screen exposure, or even the heart rate at which the first cardiovascular exercise is attempted. A helicopter parent can be taught and also encouraged by their child’s doctor to maintain strict sleep and waking schedules, specific protein-based meals, drastically reduce screen time, supervising exercise, and with a constant willingness to adjust specific circumstances of exposure to monitor recovery. The goal of medicine is to heal, and we found that healing is best accomplished by a clear adoption of the helicopter approach, albeit for a transient period. We often triumphantly declare the last visit to a concussion clinic as graduation. It marks a return to normal activities: sports, homework, time spent with friends. Their commencement to self-governance has begun. TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

Can Kevin Warsh Change the Federal Reserve?
2026-09-19

Can Kevin Warsh Change the Federal Reserve?

TIME | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings On Sept. 15 and 16, Federal Reserve Chair Kevin Warsh presided over his third Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) meeting, the body responsible for setting the interest rates that shape the American economy. With August jobs numbers coming in stronger than analysts expected, the Fed unanimously decided to raise interest rates by a quarter percentage point for the first time since 2023. Its new benchmark interest rate sits at a target range of 3.75% to 4%, and markets are expecting at least one additional interest rate hike by the end of the year. That decision unfolded against a striking backdrop: inflation has run above the Fed's 2% target for more than five and a half years. “The plain fact is that inflation is too high and has been for too long,” stated Warsh. Few economists disagreed with Warsh’s determination and the Fed’s decision—but President Donald Trump did. Trump quickly critiqued the committee’s undivided vote to raise rates via social media. “LOWER THE INTEREST RATES FOR THE UNITED STATES OF AMERICA, AND FAST!” he cried. And upon landing in North Carolina for a campaign rally, the president told reporters he blamed Warsh’s Board of Governors for the decision. “I told Kevin, I said, ‘You might as well vote with the board because it’s just not going to matter.’ The board is very hostile,” he said. “They’re very political. They’re doing the wrong thing. They’re a bunch of politicians.” Warsh took the helm of the more than century-old institution earlier this year and has moved quickly to put his stamp on it, launching five task forces within his first month, each charged with developing recommendations across a different area of the Fed's mandate. How far he can take that reform agenda will depend on his ability to build coalitions among fellow board and FOMC members and to hold the confidence of financial markets. At the press conference following his first FOMC meeting in June, Warsh announced several initiatives covering the Fed's communications strategy, balance sheet, data sources, productivity and jobs, and inflation framework. Each is led by three outside experts drawn from academia and industry. The groups are expected to deliver preliminary findings this fall, with most wrapping up by year's end. The pace and ambition of the efforts signal that Warsh is serious. Whether it proves truly transformative remains an open question. Of his five reform areas, communication has drawn the most attention, and Warsh moved on it before the task force even convened. He stopped offering long-term projections on Fed policy actions and simplified its press releases, signaling a clear break from recent practice. At the heart of this shift is Warsh's belief that markets have grown too dependent on Fed communication, paying more attention to what officials say than to the underlying economic data. He argues this creates a feedback problem: if markets are reacting to the Fed rather than to the data, the Fed gets a distorted read on where markets actually stand. Central to this critique is his skepticism of forward guidance, the practice by which Fed officials provide long-term outlooks to signal predictability and transparency. Forward guidance in various forms has been used since the 1990’s but became central as a policy tool when the policy rate reached “the zero lower bound” during the 2008 financial crisis. It was never fully retired. Warsh wants to end it, arguing that it ties the Fed's hands and leaves officials with less room to respond to new information as it arrives. "The role of forward guidance should be limited and circumscribed. Otherwise, it risks creating ambiguity in the name of clarity. Oversharing policy deliberations and overcommitting to future decisions can lead markets, businesses, and households astray,” argued Warsh. “And I believe when policymakers make quasi-commitments on interest rates through the cycle, we inhibit our own freedom to make the right calls when it's time to decide." Yet even a more restrained communication style does not insulate a Fed chair from market scrutiny. After his second FOMC meeting in July, Warsh learned that lesson firsthand. Many observers felt he underperformed at the press conference, and markets responded with alarm, raising questions about his credibility at a moment when inflation remains stubbornly high and the chair, while acknowledging the problem, has appeared reluctant to deploy his most powerful tool: raising interest rates. To be sure, Warsh is not the first Fed chair to rattle markets during a first-year press conference. Both Jerome Powell and Janet Yellen had similar stumbles early in their tenures. What matters is the recovery, and Warsh moved quickly to make one. At the annual Jackson Hole Economic Policy Symposium in Wyoming at the end of August, he delivered a forceful address on the state of the economy and the urgency of bringing inflation to heel, reassuring markets and resetting the tone ahead of September's meeting. Warsh's push to streamline Fed communication extends to the meeting calendar itself. He has proposed reducing the number of annual FOMC meetings from eight to six, which would mark the first such structural change since the 1980s. Fewer meetings, he argues, would give the committee more time between sessions to absorb incoming data and deliberate before acting. Critics counter that the tradeoff is agility. With only six scheduled meetings a year, the Fed would have fewer opportunities to respond to fast-moving economic conditions without resorting to emergency sessions and inter-meeting moves. Importantly, Warsh has not convinced critics that the current schedule leads to worse policy outcomes. Among Warsh’s remaining reform areas, the balance sheet and inflation framework task forces carry the greatest consequence. After the 2008 financial crisis, the Fed began purchasing long-term government bonds and other securities to inject liquidity into the economy, a practice known as quantitative easing. Its balance sheet swelled from roughly $1 trillion before the crisis to nearly $9 trillion in the wake of the Covid pandemic. It currently stands at $6.7 trillion, a level Warsh views as excessive. Bringing this total down is one of his stated goals, but doing so is acutely market sensitive. Any reductions will need to be phased in gradually and communicated with care, as an abrupt shift risks triggering the kind of market disruption the Fed is expressly trying to avoid. The inflation framework task force operates under similarly intense scrutiny from policymakers and markets alike. The 2% inflation target itself is not on the table. The task force will instead examine the drivers of inflation, how it is measured, and what tools are available for delivering price stability in a changing economy. The fourth task force examines the economic impact of technology, and artificial intelligence in particular, on the Fed's dual mandate of keeping prices stable and employment high. The fifth focuses on data sources, evaluating new information streams and methodological changes to improve how the Fed collects data and measures inflation. The goal is to give policymakers more current information on the state of the economy. To enact any of these changes, whether on the balance sheet, the inflation framework, or the number of FOMC meetings, Warsh will need to bring his fellow policymakers along. The chair has the authority to convene the task forces and shape their composition, but any resulting changes require approval from the seven-member board of governors, or in the case of FOMC meetings, the full 19-member committee. All proposed reforms will face intense internal debate. The recent frequency of dissents during FOMC meetings suggests that not all members will greet the reform agenda with equal enthusiasm. How transformative Warsh's tenure proves to be will therefore depend not only on the ambition of the proposed reforms but on his ability to bring a majority of colleagues on board. And as his July press conference demonstrated, financial markets will be watching and rendering their own verdict throughout. Warsh has taken over the Fed at a turbulent moment. Inflation has been running above the 2% target for half a decade. Supply shocks from the conflict in Iran and the closure of the Strait of Hormuz, combined with repeated tariff threats from the White House, have injected fresh uncertainty into the U.S. economy. Meanwhile, the effects of artificial intelligence, beyond an immediate boost to economic activity, and the potential productivity gains as well as the potential job losses, remain difficult to measure and even harder to predict. And with midterm elections approaching, political pressure is only likely to intensify. Trump has repeatedly called for lower interest rates, and he seems unlikely to quiet his calls. Faced with these pressures, Warsh has set out on an ambitious reform path. Whether he succeeds will come down to two things: the pace at which he can advance his agenda, and his ability to bring enough of his fellow Fed members with him. Most importantly, Warsh and his colleagues will need to convince the public, the financial markets, and Congress that the changes adopted will lead to better policy making. What 1,000 Women Workers Can Teach Us About Surviving Extreme Heat
2026-09-19

What 1,000 Women Workers Can Teach Us About Surviving Extreme Heat

TIME | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings On Sept. 2, 2026, the United Nations Environment Program report, Limiting Overshoot, declared that the world has fallen short of keeping global warming to 1.5°C above pre-industrial levels, and that urgent action is needed to course-correct. That same week, NPR reported that extreme heat killed nearly five times as many people in the United States as official records indicated. These findings followed two consecutively brutal summers. Death counts from heatwaves across Europe dominated news cycles this year as the continent’s aging infrastructure struggled to cool down. “Heat domes” trapped heat across the United States. But merely counting deaths from heat risks missing its impact on human life. Each heat dome in the U.S. affected over 100 million people. Among them were construction workers, outdoor workers, delivery workers in unairconditioned cabs, farm workers, the unhoused, the poor who could not afford air conditioning, those on medications on whom the impact of heat was amplified, those with mental health disorders, and those with little or no access to healthcare. According to the International Labor Organization, billions of workers around the world are exposed to extreme heat every year. While the vast majority of them do not die, many suffer deeply from the arduous ignominy of a life exposed to heat, day and night, year after year. While counting deaths from heat is an imperfect science, the quantification of heat’s impact on mental health, physical well-being, wages, food security, habitat, and infrastructure is even more complex. One thousand women working in India’s informal labor economy have set out to quantify this burden—to understand what impact the heat and humidity in their lived environments at home and at work have on their health, wages, and well-being. Partnering with our team at Harvard, the Self Employed Women’s Association in India has recruited women from trades including street vendors, waste recyclers, home-based artisans, and farmers to participate in the Community Heat Adaptation and Treatment Strategies (CommHATS) study. For 13 months, each woman is equipped with a wrist wearable, microsensors measuring heat and humidity at home and workplaces, labs measuring critical biomarkers, and surveys documenting home and work layouts, wellbeing, and wages. Considered together, this data constructs one of the world’s largest such databases of the impact of heat on human health. Building on prior work that has shown that long exposure to heat impacts multiple organ systems, including the heart and kidneys, the CommHATS project seeks to address unanswered questions in our attempts to adapt to extreme heat: How do we know if cool roofs work? What should our metric of success be? If the temperature drops a bit, but I still cannot sleep restfully, is the intervention valid? What should the threshold for a new heat insurance product be: the temperature at the airport where the weather station is, or the temperature in my home, which is actually how hot I feel? In India, where over half of women of reproductive age are anemic, how does heat further stress their hearts? Through community meetings and WhatsApp groups, research findings are regularly shared with all participants, who routinely ask how the data will help improve their lives. Early findings, which we will share at New York Climate Week, reveal devastating wage losses on hot days, shortened sleep spans, and high cardiac stress. This kind of relentless, unforgiving heat forces a different kind of reckoning that goes beyond OSHA regulations and market solutions. In places like India, where around 90% of workers are informally employed, there is no employer to hold accountable. The poor have no choice but to adapt. They change working hours at the cost of childcare. They reallocate savings to protect health and wages. They buy water jugs to carry potable water, and awnings to create shade, and ice chests to cool their produce. The woefully underfunded global financial instruments for heat adaptation have no existing structural mechanism to actually reach the most vulnerable, and so those most at risk bear the price themselves. The CommHATS project is implemented daily by grassroots workers, a testament to its ease of use. They check on the sensors and the wearables, and most importantly, the participating workers. The participants are invested in the questions, the tools, and the analysis. The tool is thus built for scale and can help communities anywhere in the world understand what their environmental exposure is at an actionable scale that is relevant to decision-making. It helps measure exposure as well as the efficacy of interventions. In our study, we find that the cost of our collective inaction is already being borne by those whose lives are already stressed. The human spirit is resilient, and perhaps life goes on. But in this warming world, life is compromised and diminished. Millions may still live through extreme heat and not yet enter the death counts, but they cannot thrive. How a New Trump Administration Rule Could Restrict Access to Green Cards—and Confront Immigrants With a Tough Choice
2026-09-18

How a New Trump Administration Rule Could Restrict Access to Green Cards—and Confront Immigrants With a Tough Choice

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump walks on his way to attend a dinner in the Rose Garden of the White House in Washington, D.C. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump’s Administration is rolling out a new rule that experts predict will make it more difficult for immigrants to get green cards if they access public assistance programs, such as Medicaid and food stamps—and could deter those hoping to obtain the status from enrolling in public benefits. The rule, which goes into effect on Friday, updates a policy dating back to the 19th century that the U.S. has long used to prohibit immigrants who are believed to be likely to become a “public charge” from getting green cards. Trump’s Department of Homeland Security (DHS) has argued that the change “is restoring the basic principle that immigrants must be able to support themselves,” adding that it is “reaffirming the requirement of self-reliance, protecting public resources, and ending policies that encouraged dependency on hard-working American taxpayers.” The rule marks the latest move in Trump’s efforts to sharply restrict both illegal and legal immigration. The President enacted a similar change during his first Administration, but the effort was subjected to court challenges and ultimately rescinded by his successor, Joe Biden. The new rule has already been challenged in court by a coalition of more than a dozen states, including New York and California. It’s unclear at this point how the ongoing litigation will affect the rollout of the rule. Here’s what to know about the new rule and how it could affect immigrants. With its new policy, the Trump Administration is implementing a change to what’s known as the “public charge” rule, a policy first established under the Immigration Act of 1882. Since then, one factor that the U.S. government has considered when reviewing green card applications is whether the applicant is likely to become dependent on government assistance. Historically, officials reviewing these types of applications have only taken into account cash benefit programs—for instance, Supplemental Security Income from Social Security—as “public benefits” that could lead to an immigrant being deemed a “public charge” and having their green card application denied. They have usually not deemed non-cash public benefits, such as Medicaid and food stamps, as programs that would disqualify an immigrant from obtaining permanent resident status. During his first term in the White House, Trump tried to expand the programs that could result in an immigrant being deemed a “public charge,” but a federal judge ruled that the policy had to be vacated, saying it had “numerous unexplained flaws” that made it “arbitrary and capricious.” When former President Joe Biden was in office, he officially rescinded Trump’s rule and stipulated that non-cash benefits would not result in an immigrant being considered a “public charge.” In July, the Trump Administration revealed that it was revoking the Biden-era policy and was rolling out a change similar to the one that faced legal challeges during Trump’s first term. Under the new rule, officials reviewing green card applications are able to consider a broader scope of public benefit programs. The Trump Administration has noted that the new “public charge” rule does not apply to some immigrants—for instance, refugees. The guidance, though, is vague—it doesn’t include a full list of the benefits that can be taken into consideration, saying only that immigration officers will be “using good judgment and discretion” and will be “making individualized, fact-specific public charge inadmissibility determinations, based on a totality of the alien’s circumstances.” “Now, the officers who are reviewing and adjudicating these cases have much more discretion in how they make that determination—whether they believe the person is likely to become a public charge,” says Melissa Shepard, the legal services director at the Immigrant Defenders Law Center. “The difference now is that it’s a much broader analysis, whereas before it was a little more limited.” Adriana Cadena, the executive director of the Protecting Immigrant Families Coalition, says she worries that, because the new policy is so broad, it “opens the door for abuses by immigration officials” during the green card application process. “It’s going from having guidance and clarity and understanding to opening the door to any kinds of programs,” she says. Immigration experts say that the new policy could result in more green card applications being rejected, as well as cause many immigrants to delay seeking permanent resident status. Charles Kuck, an immigration lawyer and former national president of the American immigration Lawyers Association, says that he expects there will be “massive numbers of denials” of green card applications as this new policy is rolled out. He adds that he and his colleagues have heard from some of their clients that they plan to wait until after Trump leaves the White House before filing their green card applications. People who do not have permanent resident status in the U.S. are not eligible for many public assistance programs, but experts also warn that the new rule could result in immigrants disenrolling from public benefit programs that they or their children are eligible for because they are afraid of potentially harming their chances of obtaining a green card in the future. Experts are additionally concerned that the Trump Administration’s new rule opens the door to immigration officials taking into account whether a green card applicant’s family member used a public benefit. The new rule stipulates that “if there is evidence in the record the alien’s family members whom the alien is legally obligated to support receive means-tested public benefits based on the alien’s income falling below the designated threshold, DHS would consider that fact.” Kuck says he fears that this could lead to a situation where, for example, if a green card applicant’s child who is a U.S. citizen receives a public benefit, such as a free school lunch program, that could lead to their parent’s application being denied. According to Shepard, it’s not yet clear how exactly the new policy will be applied in practice because it is so broad and gives immigration officers “so much discretion.” But she says she believes the change will cause people to be “a lot more cautious” and potentially fearful to access public assistance programs. Cadena says the new rule is “forcing families to choose between either being able to adjust their status in the future or be able to have access to essential programs like food and child care.” “In the current environment that we are in, where immigrant family safety is constantly being threatened, immigrant families … want to be able to keep their families together,” Cadena says. “And if keeping their child enrolled in an important program like Medicaid is going to threaten that, then they would more than likely disenroll their children from these programs.” DHS has previously estimated that the rollout of the “public charge” change could lead to more than 950,000 people disenrolling from or choosing not to enroll in public assistance programs. The change is the latest action the Trump Administration has taken to restrict immigration, including ramping up efforts to limit several legal pathways to immigration and proposing raising the fees associated with applying for citizenship. “This is just one small factor or small piece of the entire immigration system, I think, that is under attack,” Shepard says.

Why the Outcome of Russia's First Parliamentary Elections in Years Is All But Assured
2026-09-18

Why the Outcome of Russia's First Parliamentary Elections in Years Is All But Assured

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In this pool photograph distributed by the Russian state agency Sputnik, Russia's President Vladimir Putin gives a speech during a ceremony to present state awards in the defence industry sector at the Kremlin in Moscow on September 18, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Russia is just one day into three-day elections that will determine the makeup of the lower house of the country’s parliament, the State Duma. But the outcome of the vote has been all but guaranteed since before the first ballots were cast. The elections, the first parliamentary vote the country has held since its invasion of Ukraine in 2022, are widely expected to result in the Kremlin-aligned United Russia party maintaining its overwhelming dominance in the Duma. The party currently holds 310 of the 450 seats in the chamber, giving it a supermajority and the power to make major legislative decisions without the input of other parties. It needs to keep at least 300 seats to retain that supermajority. Four other political parties are also expected to be represented in the Duma following the election, all of which generally vote in line with United Russia and support the war in Ukraine. They are the Communist Party of the Russian Federation, the Liberal Democratic Party of Russia, the A Just Russia party, and the New People party. Democracy watchdogs and foreign governments have denounced Russia’s elections as being neither free nor fair. Russian officials have rejected such claims in the past, saying that the country carries out its voting process in line with the law. But experts tell TIME that the Russian government exerts its influence in ways that all but predetermine the results of elections and ensure that President Vladimir Putin and United Russia maintain control. And this year’s vote, says David Szakonyi, an expert on Russia and election fraud who is professor at George Washington University, is “occurring in the most repressive political environment in Russia's post-Soviet history.” Here's what experts have to say about how the Kremlin ensures elections result in its desired outcome—and why it continues to hold them. The Kremlin uses multiple levers to influence elections on the government level, at the polls, and in daily civilian life, according to Szakonyi. “Without a doubt, the most powerful tool” in the Kremlin’s arsenal, Szakonyi says, is limiting which candidates appear on the ballot in the first place. In the case of the ongoing parliamentary vote, the one anti-war party that was registered by the Central Election Commission, the Yabloko party, was prohibited from participating in the election by Russia’s Supreme Court. The high court ordered that the party be removed from the ballot last month in response to a lawsuit from the pro-Kremlin Rodina party that accused the Yabloko party, which has garnered support from young Russians and those opposed to the war in Ukraine, of copyright infringement, receiving foreign funding, and extremism. Szakoni says that the Kremlin has the ability to “harness the judicial system to pass rulings” such as the Supreme Court’s decision against the Yabloko party, which it uses as a “legal veneer” that shields the influence of Putin’s government over the ballot. This screening of anti-war and other opposition parties, he says, comes months in advance of elections, creating a list of candidates that includes little or no meaningful opposition at all. The Kremlin has also labeled opposition figures who oppose the war as “foreign agents,” a designation that prevents them from holding public office. In other instances, opposition leaders and activists such as Alexei Navalny have been prosecuted and imprisoned. Still others have fled the country. Experts tell TIME that the Russian government has also exercised its influence more directly on vote counts to achieve its desired election results. “Historically,” Szakonyi says, Russian electoral officials have “intervened regularly to deliver votes to the ruling party and take them away from competitors.” In the past, this was done via “crude ballot box stuffing,” he says; now, he asserts it is also performed by altering the results of online votes. Online voting is used in more than a third of Russia’s regions, which Szakonyi asserts puts more power in the government’s hands since it eliminates the physical tally represented by paper ballots. “The way that the electronic votes are being counted is almost completely a black box for the rest of society,” he says. Regina Smyth, an expert on Russia and authoritarian regimes who is a professor at Indiana University, says that “falsification,” or the practice of altering the number of votes that are counted, occurs at precinct election commissions, of which there are over 90,000, all across Russia, that she says are “mostly staffed by loyalists, and they will simply report false numbers.” There is also fraud, Smyth alleges, in “mobile voting,” when officials deliver and pick up ballots from people in their homes who are, for example, disabled or ill and unable to vote otherwise. On a deeper level, an “implicit threat” stemming from the Russian government sways countless votes each election cycle, Szakonyi contends. “Many Russian voters do not have the autonomy to cast an independent vote. They work for the state,” he says. He argues that many university students, healthcare workers, and teachers, whose livelihoods depend on state-run organizations, belong to a “block of captive, vulnerable voters” who fear prosecution by the Kremlin more than they value the influence of their ballot. Voting is also taking place this year in Russian-controlled areas of Ukraine: in Crimea, which Russia annexed in 2014, and in the Luhansk, Donetsk, Kherson, and Zaporizhzhia regions. “In the occupied territories of Ukraine, where the elections are being held in these illegally occupied territories, soldiers, heavily armed soldiers, travel from house to house coercing people to vote,” Smyth tells TIME. On top of the various methods the Kremlin allegedly uses to achieve the voting results it wants, there are slim to no oversight mechanisms in place to document election interference in the country. Golos, which was the only independent election watchdog within Russia, shuttered in 2025 following years of governmental pressure after its chairman was found guilty of working with an "undesirable organization" and imprisoned in a penal colony. Szakonyi describes Golos’ loss as the “biggest blow toward any type of accountability” for the Kremlin, saying it was “the biggest tool that civil society had to identify, uncover, and publicize the fraud that was basically happening in every single electoral cycle.” Russia has also refused to invite international election monitors from the Organization for Security ​and Co-operation in Europe (OSCE), the world’s largest regional security organization, to observe this year’s elections. Maria Telalian, director of the OSCE’s election-monitoring office, the Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights, said in a statement last month that the decision not to invite any OSCE monitors was “deeply regrettable, particularly as it continues a pattern of not implementing the commitments that every OSCE participating State has undertaken towards its own citizens and the international community.” The ongoing vote marks the third nationwide election in Russia to take place without OSCE observation. Russia previously imposed severe limits on OSCE election observation in 2021 that the organization said made monitoring that year’s parliamentary elections in the country impossible. It did not invite the group’s monitors to the 2024 presidential election at all. Despite the results of the parliamentary elections being all but determined, they carry a symbolic weight crucial to Putin’s legitimacy in the public eye and his desire to continue the war against Ukraine, experts tell TIME. “There's lots of reasons to have elections. First and foremost, the Russian population expects elections,” Szakonyi says. “Putin's legitimacy depends on his ability to win election votes to the presidency and have his ruling party win on a regular interval, and demonstrate that they're popular and that their policies are in demand from the citizenry.” During an address in which Putin urged Russians to vote days before the elections began, the leader said that “your choice and resolve will show once again that millions of people stand behind our fighters” and that “participating in the elections is our shared contribution to Russia’s victory.” “This is a way for him to theoretically show that the Russian population is united behind his war and wants to continue fighting, and that Russia has had this referendum and will continue to push harder,” Szakonyi says.
The Atlantic
Trump’s Monumental Obsession
2026-09-20

Trump’s Monumental Obsession

The Atlantic | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
Donald Trump is facing multiple challenges. The United States is losing to one of its worst enemies in a war that has left the American economy mired in the sludge of growing inflation and rising energy prices. His popularity is now hovering around second-term George W. Bush levels, and may even be headed for end-stage Richard Nixon numbers. His party faces the prospect of a crushing defeat in midterm elections that are only six weeks away. (In some places, early voting has already begun.) The country and the 47th president’s administration are in trouble on every front. Despite all of this military, economic, and political chaos, Trump has stayed true to a handful of projects: renaming the John F. Kennedy Center for the Performing Arts, demolishing the East Wing of the White House and replacing it with a ballroom that even the French kings might have thought over the top, and building a triumphal arch in front of the bridge to Arlington Cemetery whose design looks like it was found in the bottom of Albert Speer’s desk drawer. The president is very concerned about these undertakings. He is, as they say in Washington, laser-focused on them. Ordinary folks might say “obsessed,” because at times he seems unable to talk about anything else, even when asked about matters of national importance, including America’s slow-motion defeat in the Middle East. Perhaps this world of troubles is why Trump is taking refuge in plans of marble and gilt. Even an emotionally healthier man might want an occasional break from the travails of a presidency in crisis: Harry Truman would retire to the White House balcony with a bourbon; Dwight Eisenhower played a lot of golf; Kennedy and George H. W. Bush loved sailing; Ronald Reagan watched hundreds of movies in the mansion theater and at Camp David. But Donald Trump is not an emotionally healthy man, and his obsession with monuments is not a mere diversion from the pressures of the job. His most recent tirade—posted at 7:34 this morning on his Truth Social site, some six hours after his last post the night before—suggests a president who is now lost in a fantasy city in his mind, a capital where the buildings are festooned with his name, drenched in glitz, and bristling with weapons. As is often the case with Trump’s more unhinged posts, it is necessary to read his actual words to get a sense of how alarmingly strange they are: At the strong request of the United States Military, and for National Security purposes, I have agreed to convert the magnificent Triumphal Arch, planned since the Civil War Era many years ago, at the Receptive Circle adjoining the Arlington Memorial Bridge, into a top grade Military Complex/Triumphal Arch, to house, store, and have the rapid ability to use large numbers of drones, plus Snipers, on both the roof and plaza areas, and additionally have and hold large quantities of sniper ammunition in storage. Trump goes on to complain that Washington is the only capital city that does not have a “triumphal arch,” and vows that it will now have “the greatest of them all!” Trump’s pivot from a memorial to a gilded turret is at least in part a legal strategy. When Trump’s ballroom plans faced legal challenges, the president went to the Supreme Court and argued that he had the right to continue with its construction because the bunker beneath it was a matter of national security. (Other presidents have on occasion tried to circumvent checks on their power by invoking national security, but Trump has made it a routine ploy.) The Court agreed, and Trump may now be trying to squash opposition to the arch on the same grounds. This militarization of Washington ballrooms and memorials, however, is more than politics. Trump’s imaginings of castle-like defenses on the Potomac move beyond mere bad taste to a place of genuine weirdness. He does not merely imagine the new ballroom, for example, as the cap over a new, more secure presidential bunker; he imagines a space meant for parties and celebrations as a fort where he and his guests can celebrate beneath a drone port crewed by military and security personnel, including snipers. The ballroom, however, almost seems like a rational investment compared with the arch that Trump wants to build in front of America’s most hallowed ground. The entrance to the resting place of thousands of the nation’s war dead would be a “top grade Military Complex/Triumphal Arch”—whatever that means. (Perhaps it should include a portcullis and a drawbridge.) It would also be a permanent storage facility for a lot of ammunition. What could go wrong? A space open to the public would also be a continuously operating military installation. Just imagine the sense of peace that visitors, tourists, and schoolchildren would feel standing atop an ammo dump full of ordnance. But at least it might stop Chinese paratroopers or al-Qaeda frogmen from seizing Washington with a daring raid after they’d subdued suburban Virginia. None of this makes sense as a matter of national security. Yes, the White House is one of the most important centers of command and control in the United States, but it already has an array of defenses—including snipers on the roof when the Secret Service determines they might be needed. Other memorials and tourist attractions in D.C. are likewise guarded by layers of security and law enforcement. Trump, however, is imagining permanent military installations, including one on the roof of a White House annex, all because the current president apparently wants his successors to wake up every morning, see a ginormous eyesore, and remember that Donald Trump once lived there. Militarizing an arch in front of the Memorial Bridge is an even more dangerous and stupid idea than turning a ballroom into a bastion. Motorists crossing the river would have to drive around the structure, potentially creating the kind of traffic jams that terrorists could more easily exploit in a city already full of choke points. (I lived in Arlington and commuted to the District; it’s a messy trip even now.) The arch would also stand close to the approaches to Reagan National Airport, as if that overburdened facility needed more safety and security challenges. And stuffing the arch with weapons would not only contradict the notion of honoring those who have died in battle; it would increase the possibility of a mistake or a malfunction. The most important reason not to build the arch, of course, is that it is ugly and un-American. It is fascistic bombast, a militaristic yawp in front of a cemetery that is meant to be a place of respect and rest for the dead and quiet reflection for the living. It will loom over the cemetery, insisting that instead of thinking of the honored dead, Americans think only of Donald Trump. The president has never understood why Washington does not look like other capitals. America does not have a “triumphal arch” because Americans are not the kind of people who generally build such things. They do not ask their fellow citizens to swan about in uniform to celebrate foreign conquests. Napoleon broke ground for the Arc de Triomphe in Paris in 1806 to welcome his troops home after their victory at the battle of Austerlitz. It would take another 30 years to complete it, by which time Napoleon had long since returned in defeat from Moscow and then died in exile. Speaking of Moscow, most of the institutions of the Russian government are surrounded by walls and parapets. The word Kremlin comes from the Russian word for “fortress,” and although it is imposing and even beautiful in its way, it is a symbol of centuries of autocracy and paranoia. And yet Trump wants to turn the White House and an outpost on the Potomac into similar citadels, with little regard for why America’s capital city was named for one of its greatest examples of unadorned republican virtue. Trump clearly cares nothing for such symbolism. His ego wants what it wants. He lives in a city that he evidently hates and that, for the most part, hates him back. But Trump’s obsession with these vulgar memorials to his own vanity is a reminder of the darkness around him, of his hostility to democracy, his loathing of honor, and his disordered mind. Perhaps the president is looking ahead to the day he is no longer the most powerful man in the world, and it pains him to think that he would be reviled—or, worse, forgotten. If so, he seems determined to leave his mark on America’s capital even if it requires scarring it with militarized obscenities.

Pinballing

Pinballing

The Atlantic | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
I was pinballing/ slamming the back glass/ hitting every outlane/ triggering a ramp shot/ spinning red after years/ of running/nights/ of crashing/I wanted power over something/ anything/thought: what would it look like/ the glistening body/cut open/ the jewels of itself/turning? The back glass of the body/ breached? Would I come alive in the cut/you could say seeing my inside body/its wires & circuits/my spiky wrist/you could say gleeful at the sight? Say revenge/ say pitiful to be alive this way/ to have a body relax at the sight of it, you could say.
