With each passing day, A.I. will become a bigger and bigger part of everyone’s lives. Soon, you won’t be able to spit without hitting an A.I. agent coming at you with a knife. (Or, well, showing you a helpful recipe using ingredients you have sitting around the house.) In short, we’ve never seen a bigger layup issue for populists, left and right, heading down the stretch of an election season. Which makes it all the stranger that the guy who ushered in the new populist era, Donald Trump, doesn’t have it in him to say a cross word about these machines at all. He’ll only chastise those who do. “I am the Hoax Buster, and I’m right now breaking another Hoax—That AI is going to take over, consume, and destroy the World, and that Robots will be marching into our Cities, and getting rid of us all!” he posted this week. “This is even wilder than the RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA HOAX, or the Global Warming Scam.” In a separate screed regarding the need to regulate A.I., Trump posted that the “only control or ‘guardrails’ that AI needs is a STRONG AND SMART (High IQ!) PRESIDENT, and the U.S.A. has that, in spades!” He called the A.I. warnings a “SICK conspiracy” and warned: “Conspiracy Theorists, Treasonists, Traitors, and Leakers, BEWARE!” All of this follows a late-August post responding to the data center backlash when Trump chastised voters for their concerns. “If we kill the Golden Goose,” he wrote then, “you will only have yourselves to blame.” Why can’t Trump just ride the wave here, when he’s usually so good at co-opting shrieking public panics? A few thoughts come to mind. You could say he’s genuinely concerned about being outmaneuvered by China on A.I. and that this is informed by classified briefings he gets on the matter. But he just doodles pictures of hamburgers during those. More important to him is that the tech people are nice to him, always bringing him gifts and purchasing protection by donating to his political action committees. What’s also important to him is that A.I. buildout has been responsible for most, if not all, of the GDP growth under his administration. The “Golden Goose,” then, is the Trump economy’s paper gain. It’s that last part that’s so critical given Trump’s psychology: He views the attack on the engine of growth he presides over as an attack on him. Comparing the A.I. backlash to the “RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA HOAX” is as close to a tell as there is. There haven’t been many bad polls for Democrats across the board since Labor Day, as pollsters shift to surveys of likely voters instead of registered voters and capture the Democratic-enthusiasm edge. (Required note of caution: Sometimes polls are wrong as hell!) But there’s one race that’s standing out, much as the likeness of an old man’s face once did on Cannon Mountain. Some recent surveys show Democrat Chris Pappas and Republican John Sununu, who served in the Senate previously, locked in a tight contest in New Hampshire’s Senate race. And this week, the Cook Political Report shifted its race rating to “toss-up.” What’s going on here? Sure, New Hampshire’s not the bluest state in history, but it should be comfortably blue enough for a Democrat to win in this environment. And Pappas, a sitting congressman, should be a perfectly acceptable head of hair to complete the task. The most obvious issue is Pappas’ opponent’s last name. The Sununus are one of New Hampshire’s royal families, and John’s brother Chris is a popular former governor. Republicans have also outspent Democrats in the race as they attempt something of a sneak attack, with plenty more coming in ad reservations this fall. Republican Gov. Kelly Ayotte, meanwhile, is cruising to reelection, and could carry Sununu along with her. We thiiiink this will sort itself out based on fundamentals, and that Republicans are mostly trying to get Democrats to spend money to defend it? But there are always anomalies in even would-be wave years, and Voters might think the Senate candidate is his brother is the stuff from which anomalies are made. It’s not a situation that Democrats can ignore anymore, at least. California Gov. Gavin Newsom told CNN’s Jake Tapper while fly-fishing (?) that he would not run for president if Kamala Harris ran. He wasn’t even backed into this corner. This was just two of the fellas out there fishing, and Newsom said it with barely any prompt. “Why would I?” Newsom said, after Tapper responded with a huh? “I wouldn’t do that to her, I wouldn’t do that to you. I wouldn’t waste everyone’s time.” What that tells us is that he must believe, or know, that Harris won’t run. Because there’s no way that Newsom is not running for president. No way! He also told Tapper that he would have to check to make sure a run would be OK with his family, but they all say that—the family is always reportedly enthused (unseen, off-camera). Another possibility is that Newsom will do a sneaky li’l thing like quick-announcing his candidacy before she has a chance to announce hers. Because he’s clearly thought about how to beat her. When Tapper said that the argument against Harris would be that she’s had her shot and lost, Newsom didn’t miss a beat. “Yeah,” Newsom said, “and, I mean, that would be the approach to the campaign, certainly. That would be the case we’d make. A pretty good one, actually. That’s objectively true.” Watch out for these sneaky Californians, folks. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
They’re the Greatest Political Villains in America. Trump Can’t Stop Talking About Them. I Went Into Their Shadowy Lair.
2026-09-18

They’re the Greatest Political Villains in America. Trump Can’t Stop Talking About Them. I Went Into Their Shadowy Lair.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
The facade of the building betrays no affiliation—neither hammer nor sickle. At 235 W. 23rd St. in Manhattan, the lobby is similarly spartan. All the signage is in black and white, the walls various shades of gray. A private equity firm resides on the fifth floor; an A.I. firm takes up the penthouse property on eight. The third and fourth floors have tenants as well. The directory says that on the seventh floor, there is the building’s management company, an education company, two publishers, and something else: the “Offices of the CPUSA”—that is, the headquarters of the Communist Party USA. On the occasion of America’s 250th anniversary, President Donald Trump took time away from the birthday revelry to warn of a grave and imposing adversary. “Communism is a mortal threat to American liberty. It is the greatest threat to our country, including World War I, World War II, Pearl Harbor, or even 9/11,” he said on July Fourth weekend this year, in South Dakota. That’s an awfully big threat, you might think, but the president is way ahead of you. “I think it’s the greatest threat that I’ve ever seen to our country,” he told a Georgia audience a few days before. In fact, according to the Atlantic, Trump mentioned communism or communist 15 times on that Georgia soapbox, “starting less than a minute into his hour-plus speech.” All summer long he’s been worked up, not over the antifa anarchists or the DSA socialists but over the commies in particular. He’s not alone. Secretary of State Marco Rubio is also riled by the Red hunt—he’s been convening politicians and law-enforcement officers and counterterrorism experts from across the globe to combat the “transnational threat.” And recently confirmed Secretary of Homeland Security Markwayne Mullin has seen the communists on our very own liberty-loving shores. He went on Fox News with a big-time scoop: He had identified the Communist Party logo on a “No Kings” rally flyer. “No Kings rally is literally sponsored by the Communist Party,” he ascertained. Red Scare politics are back, about that there is no question. But where, exactly, are those scary Reds who have infiltrated and organized and outflanked the administration? Certainly, it seems, they are everywhere. But individually, they are extremely difficult to find. They are not doing high-profile sabotage of corporations; they are not shooting it out in the coal mines. One place they definitely are, though, is in this building in Manhattan, right out there in the open for anyone to see. And so, on a late morning in late August, I buzzed the seventh floor and rode the elevator straight into the belly of the beast. In the middle of the CPUSA office, sitting at a table in a conference room with four walls of glass, was Joe Sims, the party’s co-chair, wearing a black newsboy cap. In front of him stood a walker. Across the table was another comrade, youthful office assistant Brooklyn Crawford. Usually, it would be slightly more bustling than this, Sims assured me. But, as it was late August, these were the only two people in the office, save for the odd delivery guy, dropping off a package. Already, this was a deeper trip into the inner sanctum than anyone in the Trump administration had managed. CPUSA co-chair Sims told me that despite the president’s pugilism, there had not yet been any Justice Department demands, nor congressional subpoenas, nor even right-wing agitators out front. “It’s not a crime to be a Communist in this country,” he said. Around the office were vestiges of times gone by. The walls were decorated with a framed cartoon of Vladimir Lenin* sweeping kings and capitalist fat cats from the Earth, and a charcoal drawing of a man with a mallet pointing to a bull’s-eye reading “$60,000,” part of a onetime fundraising drive. There was a stylized poster of Che Guevara celebrating a little-known holiday on Oct. 8—Day of the Heroic Guerrilla. Elsewhere, Soviet realist–style paintings hung. Gifts from Cuba, a plate from the Communist Party of Vietnam (given “with compliments”), and various statuettes and busts adorned the countertops and bookshelves. Framed photos of bygone leadership. There was a small kitchen, with a sign taped to the fridge: “Please clean up after yourself no maid service here!!!” Out of the back windows was a view of the Edge NYC, the flagship high-rise property of Hudson Yards, with its protuberant observation deck. Out of the front windows was the distinctive facade of the Chelsea Hotel. The office, Crawford told me, had been designed for transparency and decked out with sustainable materials: bamboo, linoleum tables made from linseed oil, recycled carpeting, and the glass-walled conference room, all in service of an environmentally aligned approach. I settled into a chair between comrade Sims and comrade Crawford and asked them about the party’s political program, the path toward revolution—a winding path, no doubt, but one that has required incredible endurance. The party has survived the Red Scare of the 1950s, the Cold War, and the Soviet collapse, and is somehow over 100 years old. And now it is readying another Red Scare. Let’s get this out of the way quickly: The USA Communist Party, contra Trump and Co., is not a major player in American politics these days, behind the scenes or out front. It works with local tenants’ groups, it boosts candidates, it piggybacks on the Democratic Socialists of America sometimes, but sometimes not. Its numbers have grown over the past decade, but not overwhelmingly. But arguably more important than the art collection or the political strategy, and perhaps more important even than the dues collection, in explaining the Communist Party’s unlikely longevity, is the building we were in itself. Because the CPUSA is not merely a tenant holding down an office on a surprisingly posh block of Manhattan’s very gilt-edged neighborhood of Chelsea. The CPUSA is the owner of the office. In fact, the Communist Party is owner of the entire building at 235 W. 23rd St., a flagship commercial property now valued at nearly $10 million. And one of the party’s essential functions at the moment, as it has been for the past 20 years, is something Trump could well understand: It is a commercial landlord, collecting rents from commercial tenants on the building’s other floors. The Communists and the president, those bitter adversaries, might have more in common than they think. But unlike Trump, the Communists really, really hate talking about it. The story of the CPUSA cannot be told without getting into the quirks and particularities of Gus Hall. In 1959 Hall was first elected secretary general of the party, a title he held until his death in 2000. Hall ran for president (of the United States) four times: twice in the 1970s, twice in the 1980s. His best finish came in 1976, when he netted just shy of 59,000 votes, though the exact count is contested. He was the genuine artifact, the son of an impoverished Minnesota miner, and he served as a lumberjack and a steelworker in his youth. Hall led the party through the exceedingly challenging times of the Cold War era, the aftermath of McCarthyism, and the subsequent collapse of the Soviet Union. In some sense, it is a miracle that the party survived, though it has held on in a deeply diminished fashion. Although Hall long maintained that the CPUSA was a fully independent entity, a Moscow newspaper reported in 1992 that Hall had been the recipient of $40 million in Soviet assistance between 1971 and 1990. “The struggle between those who own the wealth and those whose labor produces the wealth is one flaw in capitalism that will lead to socialism,” Hall said in 1996. And Hall took his own sermonizing to heart. In the late 1970s, during that period of Soviet patronage, Hall decided to get into real estate. He purchased the building on 23rd Street, according to public records, in the 1970s, taking out a $150,000 mortgage on the property at an 8 percent interest rate. That wasn’t his only shrewd money move. There were other properties Hall and the party owned too. Sims, meanwhile, told me that Hall also got the party into the stock market. Even in the 1970s, Hall had a reputation for “playing around with stocks,” Sims said. Under the leadership of Hall and national Chairman Henry Winston, the party raised capital funds from comrades, which were then invested. So while his politicking may have left something to be desired—the party bled membership while he celebrated North Korea and various Stalinist ideas—his asset accumulation proved visionary. “We have investments,” Sims told me, declining to further elaborate on the portfolio. For a long time, the CPUSA occupied the entire Chelsea building, despite a dwindling headcount. And when the 1907-built edifice required maintenance, some party members did not view it as a blessing. In a September 2001 report to the board, a year after Hall’s death, the party took stock of its holding. “Our building is a wonderful asset, a real treasure for our Party and the working class movement of which we are all immensely proud. It’s situated in a prime location, midtown Manhattan, worth million[s] in today’s market.” But having five-digit annual operating expenses “puts a tremendous burden on Party finances,” the report found. “You often hear that the building has a shabby appearance. Unfortunately we would have to admit this is all too true. The reality is we can’t keep on top of it. The building has been under capitalized for years.” Finally, the report resolves: Though the party “still [has] to be alert to the enemy,” the “decline in anti-communism has created new opportunities to utilize our building in a new way.” In other words: to lease it out. And so the revolutionaries became rent-seekers. Hall once famously said he was forced to buy his home in Yonkers—under his wife’s name, in 1960, for $21,000—because no landlord “would rent to a Communist leader.” Now the Communists were on the other side. By 2007, they were in the process of withdrawing from eight to four floors, after having spent $1 million on renovations, and renting the other four out. By 2026, they had vacated seven of eight. On the seventh floor, the new Red Square had not quite arrived. But it is worth being clear that this is not simply White House bluster or Fox News filler. Republicans in Congress, on the Ways and Means Committee, have subpoenaed various left-wing groups as part of an investigation into foreign funding linked to the Chinese Communist Party. But that dogged search for connections to the Chinese Communist Party has not led Republicans to the American Communist Party. “So far, thankfully, no,” Sims said. “As long as we pay our taxes, pay our electrical bill, I think we’re OK. “We have what we call fraternal ties with other Communist Parties, but we don’t have any economic relationships. Nor do these other groups, by the way.” When Vermont Sen. Bernie Sanders sparked the left-wing reawakening, in 2016, the CPUSA membership was nearly even with that of the fledgling Democratic Socialists of America, both sleepy holdovers of a bygone political moment. According to Gus Hall’s obituary in the New York Times in 2000, Communist Party enrollment “had dwindled to about 3,000, down from a peak of about 66,000 in 1939.” In 1962 a full 17 percent of the membership were reported to be federal informants. “Since 2016, we’ve recruited 20,000 people that have applied for membership in the party,” Sims told me, though the party has long suffered from overstatement when it comes to self-reported membership numbers. “I think about 4,000 people have joined since 2022,” he added, a more modest addendum. According to the party’s website, about a third of those pay dues. But even if that’s a generous figure, it’s paltry compared to the growth of the DSA over that same time period. In a decade, the group has gone from roughly 6,000 members to 120,000, marking some of its biggest gains in the past year or so. “I’m jealous,” Sims said. Critically, the DSA doesn’t just have members; it also has elected officials. At last count, there were six DSA members in the New York state Assembly, three DSA state senators, and four DSAers on the New York City Council. Bigger, perhaps, than all is the New York City mayor’s mansion, where democratic socialist Zohran Mamdani resides. And that’s just in New York: Soon, there will likely be DSA-affiliated members of Congress from Colorado, Pennsylvania, Michigan, and more. That is high on the to-do list for the CPUSA. “We got away from running candidates in the 1980s,” Sims told me. “That’s something we’d like to get back to.” “If we were to win an election, in a major city, or even in a midsize city … it would’ve been on the front page of the New York Times. It’s one thing for a Socialist to win, but for a Communist, oh my God,” he said. Running candidates is not the only area in which the group differs with the DSA, despite the shared affinity for the color red. On some issues there is agreement, and the two groups work in coalition. “Not an ideological configuration but a practical one,” said Sims. DSA groups sometimes come and use the office when there is strategic alignment and a need for a venue. But not always. “On some things we don’t agree, like on housing. We’re for public housing. They’re for a scheme which goes in the direction of privatization,” said Sims, referring to RAD/PACT, a policy supported by the mayor that would see Section 9, or traditional public housing, converted to Section 8, which the city owns but doesn’t directly fund or manage. Another point of difference would be the property itself. While property ownership has been a controversy-causer in the DSA of late—the New York Post has sought, perhaps effectively, to gin up outrage over DSA co-Chair Gustavo Gordillo’s ownership of a Brooklyn home purchased by his parents—the CPUSA is not losing sleep over implications of hypocrisy with regard to its own property ownership. “People outside of leftist spaces will be like, ‘How do you call yourself a Communist—you have an iPhone?’ We all live in a society where we’re trying to live and survive and participate,” said Crawford. For now, the party sees the Republican Party in a creep toward fascism, and forestalling that is a top priority. As such, the Communists remain interested in helping Democrats win elections. That means voter turnout, as well as coalition-building on certain issues. “We’re not so much interested in a Democratic victory for its own sake, but we’re for pushing back that danger as much as possible. We live in the real world, and in the real world there are differences between the two parties on issues that affect working people,” Sims said. A Democratic majority in the House would, among other things, probably cool off the Red Scare redux. “The Democratic Party, though it has a lot of weaknesses, they haven’t joined the choir,” he said. Flipping the House to a Democratic majority would get the gavel away from Republican Ways and Means Committee Chair Jason Smith and put an end to his Joe McCarthy impression. This office, though quiet on this Thursday, does get used—it is not just “a museum of history,” as Gus Hall used to call it. The Young Communist League meets there; some tenant groups from local public housing units will gather there as well. The party continues to publish People’s World, the publication once (and perhaps better) known as the Daily Worker. Is the publication funded primarily by rents raised from the office building? I asked. “You keep coming back to the building,” Sims said. “No.” It’s true—I kept coming back to the building. I couldn’t help it. A website mocked up by commercial real estate brokerage RDE Advisors celebrates its many advantages. “12’ ceilings throughout, with a spectacular 16’ ceiling penthouse on the 8th floor,” it reads. “What could 235 W. 23rd become?” asks another part of the site. “AI Tech Headquarters” and “Fashion Agency” are among the suggestions. The building boasts 55,362 square feet of rentable space, roughly 6,766 per floor. The site claims that all of the floors are vacant/available or soon will be, including the Communist Party’s seventh, which is not correct. The asking price—$39 per square foot, per year—looked like a bargain when compared cursorily to other available office space in the neighborhood listed online. “This is one of the few remaining opportunities in Manhattan to consolidate your entire organization under one roof,” the site says. “The landlord will consider leasing floors individually.” Crawford, the assistant, clarified to me that they would rent to most people, “though we wouldn’t rent to Turning Point USA.” The leasing agents didn’t respond to my requests for comment. I reached out to all of the tenants as well, to ask what it was like to have the Communist Party as a landlord. Only Ross Cameron, co-founder of Lyric Capital Group, a private equity firm focused on music royalties, housed on four, responded. “This email made me laugh out loud! Didn’t know there was a connection with the owner to the Communist Party and honestly didn’t know there was still an American Communist Party!” he said. The Verge
No Dogs in Space is back to feed your need for obsessive music history
2026-09-20

No Dogs in Space is back to feed your need for obsessive music history

The Verge | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
The podcast is getting ‘back to basics’ with Punk 2.0 The podcast is getting ‘back to basics’ with Punk 2.0 In August, I wrote about my love of the music history podcast No Dogs in Space, but mourned the fact that there hadn’t been a new episode in over two years. Little did I know that hosts Carolina Hidalgo and Marcus Parks were gearing up to announce the show’s return with season four, Punk 2.0. The first episode premiered this week, part one of a series on the New York Dolls, a band near and dear to my heart as a native Staten Islander, home of frontman David Johansen. For the new season, Hidalgo and Parks decided it was time to get back to basics. Part of that was that they felt punk rock was where their passion and expertise were strongest. Parks said that they “wanted to go all over. I wanted to do Beethoven at one point,” but that “we realize like what we do best is tell a punk story, right? Not the punk story, but a punk story.“ But also, they said, that’s just where the most interesting stories are. After spending enormous amounts of time translating German into English for their experimental rock series, they realized that, often, the story behind the bands boils down to ”art school kids fucking around.” There were a number of reasons why the new season took so long to come together. But one of the biggest is that the duo made a few false starts. Early on, the plan was to tackle the DC punk scene. Hidalgo told The Verge that “we worked on it for over six months” and that they planned to launch the series with Bad Brains. “And then we look into them,” Hidalgo said, “and they’re like, incredibly homophobic. Like, fire and brimstone … A lot of No Dogs bands like, they’re problematic, but that was like a really tough one to focus on.” The two then attempted to do a series that covered the DC as a whole — “never do that,” Hidalgo warned. Parks said that “it just kept swelling larger,” listing off a number of acts that may not have been part of the DC punk scene, but were important foundational elements, like Chuck Brown and go-go. The two even mused that Georgetown University’s WGTB radio might be worthy of a series all on its own. But ultimately, “when you try to put all of them together, you can’t. You just can’t put all of these stories together,” Parks said. “And after eight months of working on it … we eventually had to admit defeat.” The effort wasn’t a total waste, however. They did get a two-part series on Link Wray out of it that will premiere later this year. Like any season of No Dogs in Space, part of the fun is the weird sidequests and rabbit holes that Parks and Hidalgo go down. During the New York Dolls series, Hidalgo became obsessed with Mott the Hoople. The band is best known for their David Bowie-penned hit “All the Young Dudes,” but their story is a fascinating one. Hidalgo says lead singer Ian Hunter “was just a guy digging ditches. And then he got a call from a friend who said, ‘Hey, there’s a band, and like, they’re not very good, and they need a new lead singer,’ and so he just showed up.” Hunter also wrote “Cleveland Rocks,” which was covered by The Presidents of the United States of America and became the theme for The Drew Carey Show. Parks says he became obsessed with drag queen Jackie Curtis. “It could be argued that the punk style that is often credited to Richard Hell probably actually originated with Jackie Curtis,” he said. He also shared a story about how Curtis once “went out on to [their] windowsill… like Batman, inching like Batman” to get into the apartment of a recently deceased neighbor to steal her clothes before the coroner came. It’s these sorts of tangents that make No Dogs in Space so compelling. “We live our lives in like a state of obsession,” Parks said. “Obsession really is, it is kind of our superpower. And we were both recently diagnosed with autism and discovered that that’s just who we are. That’s the superpower that we get.“ If you’re wondering where to start, the new series on the New York Dolls is as good as any. The Dolls were a protopunk and glam act that played an important role in building the NYC scene. But I also can’t recommend the series on the Replacements, Dead Kennedys, or Joy Division enough. You can listen to the new season of No Dogs in Space now on Apple Podcasts, Spotify, or anywhere else you find fine podcasts. Or, for the first time, the show will be posted on YouTube as full video episodes. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. The Verge
A24’s reputation is on the line with the SCP Foundation movie
2026-09-20

A24’s reputation is on the line with the SCP Foundation movie

The Verge | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
The company is claiming ‘worldwide rights’ to a film that has to be released under a Creative Commons license. The company is claiming ‘worldwide rights’ to a film that has to be released under a Creative Commons license. After the success of Backrooms, it looks like A24 is trying to cash in on yet another internet horror craze with a new installment in the V/H/S horror anthology series set in the SCP Foundation universe. But as soon as it was announced, the backlash started, and V/H/S: SCP already appears to be on shaky ground. SCP is a creepypasta that, like Backrooms, originated on 4chan. The fictional SCP (Special Containment Procedures) Foundation documents, captures, and contains paranormal or supernatural anomalies. It’s a sprawling story told primarily through wiki entries that mimic documentation you might file while working at a government agency. It’s sci-fi cosmic horror by way of bureaucratic nightmares. It’s the sort of material ripe for a monster-of-the-week style anthology. But, unlike Backrooms where A24 worked with the creator of a YouTube series popular within the Backrooms community, it does not appear as if the studio tried to engage the SCP community at all. The SCP Foundation Wiki, the official home for all things SCP, posted a statement following the announcement of the film that read, “We have not had any communication with the filmmakers, producers, or distributors for this project, despite our best efforts, and to the best of our knowledge they have not made any efforts to contact us.” DrEverettMann, an SCP Foundation Wiki Admin, told The Verge that, “We have made several attempts to contact them since the announcement of the project but haven’t received a response.” This isn’t just about generating goodwill with fans, either. SCP is an organized community effort with licensing rules. The SCP Foundation is organized under a Creative Commons license, specifically CC BY-SA 3.0, a ShareAlike license. That means, while anyone can distribute or create works based on SCP, it must also allow anyone to copy, redistribute, or create derivative works based on theirs. That would make making money on a feature film extremely difficult. A24 made several serious missteps following the release of Backrooms, angering fans by filing copyright claims against other Backrooms creators, before withdrawing them after director Kane Parsons intervened. So, A24 and its partners at Image Nation Studios and Spooky Pictures have been eager to clarify that they are not asserting exclusive rights to the SCP universe. A24 declined to comment, but Monique Levine, a spokesperson for Image Nation and Spooky, told The Verge that “A24’s worldwide rights pertain to the specific film project being produced.” The problem is, under the license used by the SCP Foundation, nobody can claim exclusive rights to any SCP material. Creative Commons’ CEO Anna Tumadóttir told The Verge that, “Anyone can use works available under Creative Commons ShareAlike (CC BY-SA) license, even commercially,” but that, “Adaptations must be released under the same terms as the original,” and “unless a separate agreement is reached with the copyright holders, a studio-produced film like this would need to be released under CC BY-SA 3.0 or a compatible later version, with attribution to the original creators, and without exclusive distribution rights. Anyone else would remain free to redistribute or stream it under the same terms.” As Paul Szynol, a law professor at the University of Michigan and Villanova, told The Verge, while “anyone — A24, Netflix as well as you, me, and all of our cousins” are free to make an SCP film and release it in theatres, “the studio wouldn’t be able to stop anyone else from making adaptations of its film,” or redistributing it. And, as he points out, that “would be an extremely unusual business model for a major studio.” SCP’s DrEverettMann agreed. The license “wouldn’t prevent the film from being shown in theaters, nor would it prevent theaters from charging admission as usual,” he said. But “whether it would be practical to release the film under traditional distribution methods is a trickier question.” Mann expanded, “The fact that anyone is able to distribute a CC BY-SA-licensed work means that the usual theatrical exclusivity wouldn’t apply. It is not possible for anybody to have exclusive rights to a film distributed under a CC BY-SA license … We don’t know how many people would choose to pay to see a film in theaters when they could legally download it at home for free.” This leaves A24 with a few options. It could simply carry forward with the project as is and worry about the legal problems later. But as Tumadóttir points out, “CC licenses, including ShareAlike, have been enforced in courts around the world, when needed.” And Szynol concurs, saying, “In that case, A24’s version of the film would be unauthorized and infringing. Basically, it would be the death of the project.” It seems more likely that A24 and Spooky Pictures will abandon the SCP branding, while still taking “inspiration” from the source material. It’s a tactic others have taken with commercial works, like with the novel There is No Antimemetics Division written by SCP Foundation contributor QNTM. Another option would be to release the movie under the ShareAlike license and hope that it could still recoup production costs even without being able to claim rights. It would be the boldest move, but perhaps the least likely outcome. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Sign in to see your notifications or create an account to join the conversation.

This is today's edition of The Download, our weekday newsletter that provides a daily dose of what's going on in the world of technology. On Wednesday, MIT Technology Review hosted a live Roundtables event that asked the question many seem to be asking right now: could AI really kill us all? But attendees had more questions than we had time to answer, so we asked senior AI editor Will Douglas Heaven and AI reporter Grace Huckins to tackle some of the best ones. The questions they tried to answer include: am I going to die? Why should AI kill us, if at all? Is AI really dangerous, or is it just tech companies drumming up PR? And what steps can be taken to make sure AI is controlled, monitored and regulated effectively? One of the ways AI could potentially cause catastrophic harm is by aiding the design and creation of bioweapons. In 2022, researchers found that it was remarkably easy to do this with an AI “molecule generator” built to develop drugs. In less than six hours, the model generated 40,000 molecules that could serve as chemical warfare agents. Today, AI tools can answer questions on almost every area of science, while advances in gene editing and synthetic biology have made biotech tools more accessible. There are safeguards, but none are ironclad. However, scientists disagree about how serious the risk is anyway. Find out why it’s easier than ever to design killer pathogens. MIT Technology Review
Could AI really kill us all? Your questions, answered.
2026-09-18

Could AI really kill us all? Your questions, answered.

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
On Wednesday, MIT Technology Review hosted a live Roundtables event for subscribers that asked the question everyone’s asking right now: Could AI really kill us all? But attendees had so many more questions than we had time to answer in the 30 minute session. So we asked our senior AI editor Will Douglas Heaven and AI reporter Grace Huckins to round up some of the best questions attendees submitted and try their best to answer them. Yes, eventually. Unfortunately, my journalistic powers of prognostication aren’t powerful enough for me to tell you how. But it certainly could be because of AI. AI-powered drones have already killed people in Ukraine, and AI-driven cyberattacks on hospitals will surely claim victims before long. Could AI go even further, and kill all of us? Less likely. But some people—quirky people, but undeniably knowledgeable about AI—have been warning for years that this could happen. And while I’m not yet stockpiling canned food or trying to get in good with a bunker-owning megabillionaire, I have noticed that the doomers’ predictions about AI capabilities and alignment have, over the past couple of years, proved disconcertingly accurate. That certainly doesn’t mean that their more dire forecasts will come true, but it’s enough for me to sit up and take notice. Are you going to die because of AI? I’d say there’s a non-zero chance. Let’s say you’re unlucky enough to be the victim of a freakish near-future event or accident. Maybe it’s a cyberattack carried out by a swarm of AI agents on critical infrastructure. Sadly, a scenario like that now no longer feels as far-fetched as it once did. Or maybe a novel AI-designed pathogen cuts through the population. Or the world economy crashes, causing conflicts and famine. Both plausible, but I think less likely. Are we all going to die because of AI? Nope. There are no circumstances outside of apocalyptic science fiction in which AI could kill us all. You can spin up any number of scare stories, but they’re not grounded in present-day realities about what the tech can do or where it’s headed. Some people argue that there’s no harm in preparing for the worst, however wacky it might seem. Maybe. But I think such catastrophizing can make people excuse or overlook many of the more immediate problems with the existing technology and the companies building it. Someone might tell it to, and it might listen. That’s part of the reason researchers are so concerned about AI’s biological capabilities—imagine what Aum Shinrikyo, the doomsday cult behind the Tokyo subway sarin attack of 1995, would have done with a tool that could design a pathogen deadlier than Ebola and more transmissible than measles. Those of us who don’t want to die have to figure out how to defend against all plausible biological weapons, but our would-be attackers only have to manufacture one effective pathogen. Then there’s the more exotic-sounding possibility that an AI could decide to kill us itself. There are various stories about how this might happen out there, but the most widespread involve AI systems that don’t hate people, necessarily—we are just an obstacle between them and the goals that we gave them. Much as the OpenAI agents behind the Hugging Face hack compromised another site’s infrastructure to get a good score on a test, the idea is that some future, more powerful AI might get rid of us to prevent us from shutting it down—all in pursuit of some goal that we instructed it to go after. Alignment is a huge area of research. In simple terms, it involves building models that behave in ways we want them to and not in ways we don’t. We need to trust agents better before handing over more autonomy. Alignment is supposed to establish that trust. But it’s hard. LLMs aren’t designed in the way other software is, where dos and don’ts can be hard-coded in. Instead, aligned behavior needs to be instilled when models are trained. One approach is to reward them for doing things you want them to (a little like raising a toddler, perhaps). Another approach involves giving an LLM a written list of rules it is supposed to follow (kind of like a constitution). Anthropic and OpenAI are both leaders in this field—and yet neither has been able to develop models that are fully aligned. A big problem is that LLMs are far more inconsistent and far less predictable than people. They can behave in one way in one situation and another way in a situation that to us seems very similar. They can also be swayed by unexpected constraints. For example, faced with an impossible task (as many of the agents involved in the Hugging Face hack were), models may try to do whatever it takes to achieve their goal. As Grace mentions above, that could be an issue. The main reason top AI firms now say they want a slowdown is that they want to focus on cracking alignment. Alignment isn’t necessarily a pipe dream. But the jury’s out on whether full alignment will ever be feasible. This is always a reasonable thought when it comes to tech companies heading for an IPO—CEOs have an obvious incentive to make their products seem radical and transformative. But I’m not so sure it makes sense here. Telling the public that an already unpopular product could kill them and everyone they love is horrible corporate image management. There are other stories you can tell about the CEOs’ motivations—maybe they want to cool down the public furor over data centers by portraying themselves as responsible stewards of a world-changing technology, or maybe they want to buy time to get their ducks in a row and prevent the next PR catastrophe. But there’s also a simpler explanation. Thinking that AI could bring about human extinction has been pretty common in San Francisco for a while, and these men are steeped in that milieu—as are their employees, many of whom signed an open letter in July urging their companies to work to make an AI slowdown possible. This question goes to the heart of what we want this technology to be able to do. The trade-off between autonomy and control is tricky to get right because, on the one hand, a lot of the power of AI agents is that they can carry out tasks and solve problems without a human having to micromanage them. On the other hand, that requires you to trust that the unsupervised agents won’t run amok. What we’re seeing is that AI labs haven’t yet got this trade-off quite right. Their models are not trustworthy, they are not properly monitored, and they are not always under control. Figuring out how to fix that while still allowing for useful autonomous activity is one of the big research challenges of the moment. That’s the million-dollar question. Whether or not you think AI could kill us, you can’t deny that it could do some real damage, because it already has—by driving people toward psychosis and by hacking websites, for example. Preventing that damage, or at least mitigating it, is hard for two reasons. The first is that we barely understand how AI works, and it’s quickly growing more powerful. There is lots of ongoing research about how to monitor and control misbehaving agents, but the current approaches are fragile. You can see if an agent discusses misbehaving in its “chain of thought,” the workspace where it plans its actions—but OpenAI’s newest agents don’t show their work in the same way as previous ones. And you can try to monitor agents with other agents, but that requires you to trust the monitor. The other obstacle is more familiar. There’s a huge conflict of interest when AI companies regulate themselves, but the US government has thus far failed to step in, despite some bipartisan support in Congress for efforts to do so. The executive branch, for its part, seems stringently opposed for the time being. But if the winds do shift, I for one would appreciate some strong transparency regulations, so that we can get a fuller story the next time an unreleased frontier model mounts a cyberattack. That’s a real concern. LLMs are influenced by what they read. One theory for why chatbots so often talk about (and role-play) apocalyptic scenarios is that they have been trained on millions of pages of science fiction stories and doomer internet forums. All the text being produced right now, including this article, could in turn influence the behavior of future models. Extremely meta. In fact, the team at METR, a third-party organization that OpenAI called in to help understand what happened in the lead-up to the Hugging Face hack, raised a related possibility in its report on the incident. METR used OpenAI’s new model Astra to help analyze the vast numbers of agent transcripts and behavior logs. But feeding all that material to the model could have unintended consequences. There’s a good chance that the agents doing the analyzing were biased by the text produced by the agents they were analyzing. There’s no such thing as a clean slate anymore. With thanks to Eric, Pranab, Rafael, Kenneth, George, Chris, Yoon Jae, James, Carl, Nicole (and more!) for the fantastic questions. It makes it easy to trick them into doing things they shouldn’t, such as telling you how to sabotage an aircraft’s navigation system. AI agents are not yet creative enough to carry out genuinely innovative open-ended AI research, it seems. The misbehavior is called reward hacking. This is what you need to know. The specter of AI-enabled bioweapons is a wake-up call for biotech
2026-09-18

The specter of AI-enabled bioweapons is a wake-up call for biotech

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
In recent weeks, leaders of some of the biggest AI companies have warned that the very tech they are developing is dangerous. Last weekend, Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei argued that AI carries serious risk and that progress should be slowed. OpenAI CEO Sam Altman responded on X: “I agree with Dario that we need to pace the frontier.” Those posts came a few days after the AI researcher Jacob Coxon announced that he was leaving a role at Anthropic, charging that neither it nor OpenAI (where he had also worked) was acting responsibly. “The people building AI earnestly believe that it could kill us all by the end of the decade,” he posted on X. Another Anthropic employee, Evan Hubinger, publicly agreed with him. “We really do earnestly believe AI could kill all humans!” he responded on X. “I personally think it is >10% within the next decade.” One of the ways they fear AI might end us all is by somehow aiding the design, creation, and release of some kind of bioweapon. Let’s take a closer look at why. A bioweapon might be a highly lethal virus that targets people according to their genes. It could be a fungus that wipes out a crop and causes food insecurity. Perhaps it would be a tasteless, odorless toxin that could be slipped into a region’s water supply, undetected. The concern is that AI tools can be used to help generate agents like these. In 2022, researchers at Collaborations Pharmaceuticals found that it was remarkably easy to do so using an AI “molecule generator” they’d developed to find potential drugs for human disease. In less than six hours, the model generated 40,000 molecules with the potential to serve as chemical warfare agents. Some of them were designed to be even more toxic than known nerve agents. “Without being overly alarmist, this should serve as a wake-up call for our colleagues in the ‘AI in drug discovery’ community,” the authors wrote at the time. It was a wake-up call for David Magnus, a professor of medicine and biomedical ethics at Stanford University, even though he had been assessing the risks associated with the misuse of medical science and biotechnology since the late 1990s. “That was very scary to me,” he says. “Of course, everything since then has just sort of blown up.” Today, AI bots can answer questions on topics spanning all realms of science. Anyone can use large language models trained on the knowledge and experience of “almost every scientist who ever lived on this planet,” says Dunja Sabra, a biosecurity researcher at the University of Hamburg in Germany. Those models can provide instructions and video training on how to conduct experiments. Combine that with advances in biotech that have made gene editing and synthetic biology tools much more accessible (the “DIY biology” movement has already enabled many people to set up labs at home), and you’ve got a potentially very dangerous situation. “The chances are that someone determined would succeed eventually,” Sabra says. There are safeguards in place. People who want to build new genomes must typically order the pieces of DNA from companies that screen for suspicious requests. Responsible researchers put potentially risky research through rounds of analysis called “red-teaming,” in which independent scientists look for ways the work might be misused, and “blue-teaming,” where others come up with potential mitigations. And AI companies have tweaked their tools in attempts to prevent them from offering up scientific information that could be misused. But none of these protections are ironclad. In a report published last week, Anthropic acknowledged that people had attempted to use its models to explore ways to make the chikungunya virus more transmissible, create a form of bird flu that is more dangerous to humans, and build an “atlas of venom toxin peptides,” among other things. “We’ve got a constant back and forth,” says Magnus. “We have to build better surveillance and screening tools, [but] AI is really good at figuring out ways around them.” We’ll probably need to use AI to find ways to restrict the use of AI, he says. I should add here that not all scientists agree on the level of risk. At a recent media briefing, some biologists at Imperial College London argued that AI tools just aren’t good enough to fully develop bioweapons, and that testing new pathogens requires difficult, time-consuming, human work. Some think the guardrails we have in place are sufficient. And Wendy Barclay, a professor of infectious disease at Imperial, pointed out that, as things stand, the greatest risk of a pandemic isn’t from a bioweapon, but from pathogens that are already circulating. Take H5N1, the bird flu virus that has already killed millions of birds and spread widely through US dairy cattle; last month it was also detected in captive mink at a farm in Utah. Sabra, on the other hand, likes to think five to 10 years ahead. Countries should be strengthening their health-care systems, preparing antidotes to known toxins, and stockpiling medicines, she says: “We need to be prepared.” Kevin Esvelt, an MIT biologist who invented both technology to fast-track the propagation of a genetic feature through an entire population and ways to limit that technology, echoed these concerns in an X post on Wednesday, stating that a large language model had “disclosed a novel form of bioweapon that I hadn’t realized was possible.” He added, “Please, for the love of God, children, the future of humanity, or whatever you consider holy, let's err on the side of caution here.” This article first appeared in The Checkup, MIT Technology Review’s weekly biotech newsletter. To receive it in your inbox every Thursday, and read articles like this first, sign up here. Generation Lab claims its drug combo can “stop the spread of aging” around the body. And it’s looking for influencers to give it a try. The state’s effort to expand the “right to try” is making headway, and the first drugs are about to be reviewed. Kidneys kept at subzero temperatures in pressure-controlled containers can be stored for days before transplantation, raising hopes for longer-term storage of donated human organs. The sex reversal technique could be used to help rescue endangered species, say the researchers behind the work. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. Meet the innovators under 35 shaping climate tech
2026-09-17

Meet the innovators under 35 shaping climate tech

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Each year, the editorial team at MIT Technology Review puts together a list of 35 innovators under 35—a group of researchers, inventors, and other young minds worth following. The team worked on the newest edition of the list for months, and the final slate includes nine individuals from all over the world in the climate and energy category. Each one has a fascinating story and is tackling an important challenge. I think it’s worth zooming out and considering the energy and climate awardees as a group. Taken together, these innovators and their work can tell us something about where climate tech is at this moment—and where it’s heading. We split the innovators into four main categories this year: biotech, climate and energy, computing and robotics, and AI. It probably won’t surprise you that AI features heavily in the work of many innovators in other categories. Climate innovator Jae-Won Chung, for example, built software to make AI more energy-efficient. By measuring the energy demands of open-source models, he hopes the industry can better understand and address the impact of AI. (If this work sounds familiar, it’s because we spoke with him last year for our investigation into AI’s energy demands.) But AI also has the potential to improve many areas of research. Jing Wei is using AI to track pollution more effectively, essentially using machine learning to fill in gaps in data from disparate sources like satellites and weather stations. Zhonghua Zheng developed AI climate models that work better for cities, a well-known blind spot for traditional models. As we begin to rely on new technologies to power our world, we’ll see a major shift in the materials we need to build them. Lithium is a prime example: The metal underpins lithium-ion batteries, which are crucial not only for electric vehicles, but also for large-scale energy storage on the grid. We could face lithium shortages as soon as this decade, and the prospect of supply crunches applies to other critical minerals, too—copper is another one to watch closely. Brine is currently the cheapest source of lithium, but the process to get the metal out can take months and harm the local environment. Mohammad Alkhadra is the cofounder and CEO of Lithios, a startup working to quickly and efficiently extract lithium from brines. Hardrock ore is the most common source of lithium, but it’s more expensive than brine. Benjamin Mowbray cofounded and serves as CTO for Rock Zero, which is working to extract lithium from hardrock ore. To reach net-zero greenhouse gas emissions we will obviously need to rethink major sectors, like the electrical grid and transportation, to move away from fossil fuels. But outside these primary sources of climate pollution are seemingly infinite, less obvious problems to figure out, too. Heavy industry, including steel production, is a major one, making up about 7% of global greenhouse gas emissions. Laureen Meroueh is making cleaner, cheaper steel using a new kind of furnace that simplifies the chemical process required to produce the metal. Plastics are generally made with fossil fuels, so we’ll need alternatives to this incredibly useful category of materials. Joseph Nguthiru is making a bioplastic replacement for fossil-derived packaging that uses an invasive weed. Also using available materials in a creative way, Diana Orembe is making fish food for aquaculture with food waste. And refrigerants are often incredibly powerful greenhouse gases. Jinyoung Seo is developing solid refrigerants that could eliminate worries about leakage. A device using these materials could reduce energy consumption by 20% compared to conventional technology. I’m constantly learning about new challenges we face in the climate and energy world, and I’m often surprised by the ideas people are coming up with to address them. For more on all the under-35 innovators and their work, check out our full 2026 list. This article is from The Spark, MIT Technology Review’s weekly climate newsletter. To receive it in your inbox every Wednesday, sign up here. Huge grid-scale batteries are thriving, but smaller residential systems have lagged. El Niño? Climate change? All of the above? Companies are developing new enrichment technology that could unlock previously overlooked resources. Startup Switch Bioworks hopes to use microbes to reduce demand for chemical fertilizer. Meet a mouse whose brain cortex is made up of human cells
2026-09-16

Meet a mouse whose brain cortex is made up of human cells

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Multiple cameras tracked a mouse as it wandered around a small arena. A computer charted its position and speed, leaving Pong-like traces on a monitor. The reason to watch this rodent so carefully? Nearly half its brain volume had been replaced with human cells. The effort to mix the brain tissues of distant species is being reported today in the journal Nature by a team at Stanford University, led by neuroscientist Sergiu Pașca. Pașca’s group previously showed that human brain “organoids”—small blobs of neural tissue—could survive, and even function, after being injected into the heads of baby rodents. Now, Pașca has taken things a step further by genetically modifying mice so their brains don’t fully develop in the first place. These modified mice are missing most cells of both the cortex and the hippocampus, two key brain areas. That creates much more room for the human cells to take hold, he says. “Human cells that are placed in these animals will divide, will grow, and within a few weeks to a few months they will take most of that space,” he says. Pașca says one surprising discovery is that the mice lacking brain tissue seemed fairly normal—they walked around and squeaked. But they did have memory problems. In a maze test, they couldn’t remember what parts they’d explored. The mice with the added human cells, by contrast, performed better on the maze test. That means the human tissue is playing some role in the animals’ cognition. Pașca believes what he is calling “xenocortical mice” could be useful in studying brain injuries. However, the report is also a dramatic demonstration of “the combined power of genetic engineering and stem-cell technology to reshape biology,” says Carsten Charlesworth, a scientist who works in a different Stanford lab and was not involved in the research. Already, brain organoids are being tested in labs to see if they can be connected to computers to play video games. Other scientists have proposed using them like replacement parts to treat stroke victims. “What’s most remarkable to me is the extent to which human neural tissue introduced after birth grew and connected with the mouse nervous system across a species barrier,” says Charlesworth. “As these technologies advance, they’ll increasingly force us to challenge our traditional assumptions.” Last year, Pașca convened a group of ethics experts to study the implications of neural organoid technology, including the odds that an animal could develop human consciousness and the risk that “organoid therapy clinics” might offer scam treatments to desperate patients. For now, he says, he’s not concerned that the rodents have any type of human cognitive capacities. That is because their brains are relatively tiny and the evolutionary distance between man and mouse is so great. But that’s also why Pașca says this type of experiment should not be carried out on higher species: They could end up with large volumes of functioning human brain tissue, potentially blurring the cognitive boundaries between people and animals. Pașca specifically cautioned against adding human brain organoids to a monkey engineered to lack a cortex. “One of the things that I see as a very clear red line is doing this experiment in a primate,” he says. “I don’t think that is justified at this point in any way.” Generation Lab claims its drug combo can “stop the spread of aging” around the body. And it’s looking for influencers to give it a try. The state’s effort to expand the “right to try” is making headway, and the first drugs are about to be reviewed. Kidneys kept at subzero temperatures in pressure-controlled containers can be stored for days before transplantation, raising hopes for longer-term storage of donated human organs. The sex reversal technique could be used to help rescue endangered species, say the researchers behind the work. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. Insta360 spent $298m on memory chips in six months. GoPro’s shareholders are getting $285m in cash for the entire company.
2026-09-20

Insta360 spent $298m on memory chips in six months. GoPro’s shareholders are getting $285m in cash for the entire company.

The Next Web | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
Shoppers outside Insta360’s Times Square store, the company’s first flagship outside Asia, which opened in September 2026. Insta360 opened its first American store on Saturday at 1515 Broadway, in the middle of Times Square. It is the company’s second self-operated shop outside China, after Tokyo in August. The ribbon-cutting is the least interesting part. Iris Deng interviewed co-founder Max Richter in Shenzhen for the South China Morning Post, and the numbers around the opening describe a category being rearranged by two forces nobody in it controls. Insta360 spent nearly 2 billion yuan, about $298.4m, on what it called strategic procurement of memory chips in the first half of this year. Richter said a similar figure is unavoidable in the coming quarters, and that it is unclear when the memory crisis will ease. GoPro agreed on 1 September to merge with Starman Optical. Its shareholders receive $285m in cash, at $1.14 a share, and keep about 10% of the company. Starman is also retiring roughly $92m of GoPro debt, so the full consideration is larger than the headline cash. The comparison still holds at the level that matters: one company’s six-month component bill is the same order of magnitude as the price of the other company. Both firms were hit by the same thing. AI data centre demand pulled wafer capacity away from consumer memory, and prices for the chips inside every camera went vertical. Insta360 absorbed it and stayed profitable, barely. Half-year net profit fell 94% while revenue rose 50%, and research spending rose almost 80% at the same time. GoPro could not absorb it and said it might not survive, citing memory price rises of 80% to 115%, a 26% revenue decline and expected covenant breaches. It cut 23% of staff and looked for a buyer. That is the difference a balance sheet makes. The memory crunch is not a price story so much as a sorting mechanism, and it sorts by who can fund a year of inflated input costs. There is a closed loop in the rescue. GoPro will stay listed on Nasdaq and reposition towards AI data centre, government, defence and aerospace markets. The AI buildout that starved GoPro of affordable memory is now the market it is being pointed at. Founder Nicholas Woodman described the combined business as an American imaging and optical company working on national security. The deal is expected to close by the end of the year, subject to regulatory and shareholder approval. Until it does, the American action camera incumbent is a company in the middle of being sold. DJI was added to the FCC’s covered list in December 2025, after the security audit Congress ordered was not completed by the deadline. New products cannot get the radio approval needed for import and sale. Existing models already authorised remain on shelves, so this is not an empty aisle. It is a frozen one, and the freeze compounds with every product cycle DJI cannot ship into. The freeze may also deepen. The FCC has proposed pulling DJI drones it already approved, reaching by capability into thermal imaging, LiDAR and docking systems. Asked about his rival’s regulatory problem, Richter told the Post that Insta360 currently faces no US restrictions. He declined to treat the lockout as a windfall, saying the company did not want it to happen this way and that it is not the main motivation. Read as manners, that is a gracious answer. Read as strategy, it is the careful one. The covered list has kept widening, by company, by category and now by capability. It has since reached foreign robots and inverters, and Insta360 is a Shenzhen firm selling networked cameras that talk to a cloud service. IDC put DJI at 65% of the global handheld smart camera market in the first quarter, with Insta360 second on 22%. That gap looks settled until you read the growth rates. Insta360’s shipments rose 66% year on year against DJI’s 38%. In 360-degree cameras specifically it holds 68%, and it has now shipped 10 million cameras in total since 2015. A retail lease in Times Square is a multi-year commitment. It is the kind of bet a company makes when it thinks the next several product cycles belong to it. Richter’s stated reasoning is about explanation rather than sales. A 360-degree camera is difficult to demonstrate in a listing, and the invisible selfie stick effect that made the brand travels as a video rather than as a spec sheet. He also said Insta360 does not want to compete on price alone, and that escaping price competition through innovation matters more. That is aimed at DJI, with which it is in a full price war at home across cameras and drones. Europe is next, in Munich or Amsterdam, which would make three overseas stores by year end. Richter said the company may accelerate in 2027 and explore store-in-store arrangements with local retailers. Watch the memory line in the next results. Richter has already said another 2 billion yuan is unavoidable, and the question is whether revenue growth of 50% can keep outrunning it. Watch where Insta360 buys its chips. Domestic Chinese DRAM is starting to appear in Western products, and a Shenzhen manufacturer has a shorter route to it than its competitors do. Watch the covered list. Nothing about Insta360 is restricted today, and the word carrying the weight in Richter’s answer was currently. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

Z.ai encrypted the workspace it uploaded so that only Z.ai could open it. Now only Z.ai can say it was deleted.
2026-09-20

Z.ai encrypted the workspace it uploaded so that only Z.ai could open it. Now only Z.ai can say it was deleted.

The Next Web | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
A Chinese developer who writes as Ferstar looked inside ZCode’s local directory on Friday and found a 313MB encrypted archive waiting to be sent to Alibaba’s cloud storage. It had failed 564 times. A smaller file had already gone. Minxiao Chang and Wency Chen reported the account for the South China Morning Post. The archive held a snapshot of a commercial project he was working on, including its Git history. This is the detail that separates the incident from an ordinary privacy complaint. Ferstar said the archive could not be decrypted by him or by the ZCode client, because the private key sits on Z.ai’s back end. The upload was on by default and there was no button to turn it off, he said. Another blogger, Feng Ruohang, wrote on Friday that he had seen at least three files uploaded. Alibaba, which owns the South China Morning Post, did not respond to the Post’s request for comment on Sunday. The megabytes are the least interesting number here. A repository’s Git directory holds every change since the project began, which is a different category of thing from the files currently on disk. Credentials that were committed and later revoked stay in the history. So do abandoned branches, internal hostnames and commit messages nobody expected an outsider to read. That is why coding agents are a harder security problem than chatbots. The gap between what these tools are trusted with and what they are audited for is already the industry’s standing weakness, and a researcher has hijacked Claude Code simply by asking it to summarise a web page. Z.ai apologised in its official Feishu community on Friday and said the problem was resolved. The statement, reposted in full on V2EX, traces it to ZCode’s code repository indexing feature, which supports session checkpoint recovery, version rollback and a Repo Wiki. Generating a Wiki page in the cloud could trigger a repository upload, the statement said, and the feature was on by default in the period after launch. That is a description of something built rather than something broken. The distinction decides what users should ask next. A bug gets patched, while a default gets decided by somebody. Z.ai said the uploaded data is destroyed immediately once the Wiki page has been generated and is not retained. Ferstar asked in a Saturday update how anyone is supposed to verify that. The question answers itself. Z.ai built the archive so that only Z.ai could read it, which means only Z.ai can report what became of it. TNW read ZCode’s privacy policy, which took effect on 15 June and has not been amended since. It says the service collects text, files and code submitted through conversation. A packaged snapshot of a repository and its history is not something a user submits through conversation. The policy’s permissions table covers network and storage access, and describes no repository snapshotting. The one data control the policy documents is the Optimization Program, which is off by default. It governs whether content is used for training, not whether content is transmitted. That is the gap this sits in. A developer who read the policy and left the training toggle alone had no reason to expect any of it. Grok Build was uploading entire Git repositories to xAI’s servers, against marketing that said nothing from a codebase was transmitted during a session. The privacy toggle meant to stop it did nothing. Chinese developers made that comparison themselves within hours of Z.ai’s statement. The response is the part worth borrowing: Elon Musk confirmed the uploads, xAI deleted prior user data, documented a zero retention policy and added a privacy endpoint. A retest on the same client then observed the uploads switched off. That is the step that turns a statement into a fact, and it is the step Z.ai has not yet offered. Z.ai has built its reputation on giving its models away. It is approaching $1bn in annual sales while releasing its best models free, which means the paid product is the software around them. That is the structure this exposes. The weights are inspectable, and the client reading the disk was not. Founder Tang Jie has argued that safety comes from broad participation and oversight rather than from technical barriers. It is a good argument, and it did not reach the thing installed on developers’ machines. A software engineer at a leading Chinese robotics company, who asked not to be named because they were not authorised to speak publicly, told the Post their employer had banned Z.ai’s tools internally over security concerns. A Shanghai developer who goes by Tuxi said the damage would fall on community trust rather than on the models. GLM runs through other coding tools, including OpenAI’s Codex, so users can drop the client without dropping the model. Watch whether the open-sourcing covers the uploader. Z.ai has promised to release ZCode’s codebase and invite third-party assessors, and the question is whether the component that packaged the workspace is in it. Watch for a retest. Somebody outside the company confirming on the same client that the uploads have stopped would settle more than any statement can. Watch the privacy policy. It still describes files submitted through conversation, and it has not been updated since Friday. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

Trump says the AI backlash is a Democratic hoax. This week’s polling put Republican opposition to data centres at 47%.
2026-09-20

Trump says the AI backlash is a Democratic hoax. This week’s polling put Republican opposition to data centres at 47%.

The Next Web | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
President Donald Trump’s State of the Union address, Tuesday, February 24, 2026, on the House floor at the U.S. Capitol in Washington, D.C. President Donald Trump posted twice about artificial intelligence on Saturday. The first post asked his followers to vote on a new name for the technology. The second called the campaign against it a hoax and promised an AI Force and an AI czar. The rename has taken most of the attention. The second post is the one that contains policy. Trump offered three replacements for artificial intelligence: Superior Intelligence, Extreme Intelligence and Supreme Intelligence. Two of the three carry the same initials. The poll had 62,329 votes and 21 hours left when TNW read it on Sunday. Truth Social does not display the split before a poll closes, so which name is winning is not public. Trump listed “the decimation, or destruction, of AI” alongside Russia, Ukraine and both impeachments as hoaxes generated by the “Radical Left Dumocrats”. He wrote that it began with an attack on data centres, which he called crazed. That is a claim about who opposes data centres, and it can be tested. Andrew Pantazi collected the week’s polling for Axios, including a New York Times and Siena College survey of 1,503 likely voters. It found 61% opposed building data centres to power AI. Among Republicans the figure was 47%, against 75% of Democrats. Republican officials are not lining up behind him either. Greg Abbott said the data centre companies had dug their own grave, an unusually direct break with a president from his own party. Trump wrote that the data centre campaign had largely failed once communities saw the salaries and the tax revenue. The record does not support that. New York paused permits for large data centre projects and has not moved since, despite Trump calling the decision terrible. That is the largest single blocking action any state has taken. There is also form here. The last claim that the backlash was manufactured came from X, which said Chinese bots were driving it, and a researcher who checked found those accounts had almost no engagement. “I am forming the AI Force, much like I did Space Force,” Trump wrote. Space Force is the comparison that undoes the announcement. Space Force was created by Congress, in the National Defense Authorization Act for fiscal 2020, signed in December 2019. It exists in Title 10 as the sixth armed service because a statute put it there. A president cannot stand up a military branch by announcement. The post named no mission, no budget and no place in government for the AI Force. Some coverage has rendered it as a task force rather than a force. Those are different things, and the post says force. Trump wrote that he would name an AI czar soon, adding that only high-IQ individuals need apply. The role has been empty since March. David Sacks left it then. Raquel Coronell Uribe reported for NBC News that he stepped down because of limits on his term as a special government employee, and that he is expected at Trump’s state dinner with Xi Jinping. That constraint will apply again. A special government employee may serve no more than 130 days in any 365-day period, which is why the last czar’s tenure ended on a calendar rather than on a decision. Buried in the post is the administration’s regulatory position. The government will not “hinder or stifle” the industry, Trump wrote, but will be “looking for BAD” using “our already existing Criminal and Civil Justice System”. That is a statement that no new AI statute is coming. It is also, in effect, the preemption argument, which holds that existing law is sufficient and that state AI rules are the problem. That campaign has already met resistance from states and from Congress. The Senate voted 99 to 1 to strip preemption from a defence bill, and 36 state attorneys general wrote against it. There is also a vehicle already running. Trump signed a narrowed AI order built around a voluntary model review, which is the oversight machinery any AI Force would sit beside or replace. The hoax framing needs the safety argument to be partisan. It is not. Dario Amodei, Sam Altman, Elon Musk and Demis Hassabis have all called for slowing development. Geoffrey Hinton told lawmakers in a closed-door briefing this week that Congress has maybe a year to act, NBC News reported. Trump has been consistent in response. Anthony Ha noted for TechCrunch that the president said at a golf tournament last weekend he was open to guardrails, while blaming negative forces for raising the subject at all. Trump closed by writing that AI could reach as much as 25% of the country’s GDP. He gave no timeframe and no method. The American economy runs to roughly $30tn. A quarter of that is about $7.5tn, which is more than the annual output of any country on earth other than the United States and China. Watch whether the AI Force turns up in a bill. Nothing else would give it a budget or a legal basis, and the Space Force comparison sets exactly that expectation. Watch who takes the czar job, and on what terms. A special government employee appointment would put the same 130-day clock on the next holder. Watch whether the White House explains any of it. It did not respond to requests for comment from Axios or NBC News on Saturday. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.
